Principal 2-bundles and quotient 2-stacks
Elena Caviglia

Abstract
. We generalize principal bundles and quotient stacks to the two-categorical context of bisites. We introduce a notion of principal 2-bundle that makes sense for a 2-category with finite flexible limits, endowed with a bitopology. We then use principal 2-bundles to explicitly construct quotient-pre-2-stacks, which are the analogues of quotient stacks one dimension higher. In order to perform this construction, we prove that principal 2-bundles are closed under iso-comma objects and we restrict ourselves to ( 2 , 1 ) (2,1) -categories. Finally, we prove that, if the bisite is subcanonical and the underlying ( 2 , 1 ) (2,1) -category satisfies some mild conditions, quotient pre-2-stacks are 2-stacks.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
18F20, 18F10, 18N10, 14A20

Introduction

In this paper, we generalize principal bundles and quotient stacks to the two-categorical context of bisites. This is the generalization to one dimension higher of the theory of generalized principal bundles and quotient stacks that we developed in
[
5
]
in the categorical context of sites. Both these theories will also appear in our PhD thesis
[
7
]
.

Principal bundles and quotient stacks are key concepts in geometry and topology. Their importance is due, above all, to their fruitful connections with cohomology groups. Given a nice topological space
X
and a group
G
, the first cohomology group
H^{1}(X,G)
of
X
with coefficients in
G
corresponds to the set of isomorphism classes of principal
G
-bundles over
X
. And, as also pointed out by Lurie in
[
12
]
, the approach of principal
G
-bundles turns out to be very useful and more effective than the standard one involving cocycles, especially when considering an arbitrary topological space. The collections of principal
G
-bundles over different topological spaces can then be captured and studied together thanks to the classifying stack
\mathcal{B}G
, which is the archetypal quotient stack.

One of the main motivations that brought us to study categorical generalizations of principal bundles and quotient stacks is the possibility to use such new generalized concepts as tools for the study of cohomology groups in new and broader contexts. We believe that this will have many interesting applications both in mathematics and in mathematical physics.

Principal bundles over topological spaces and over smooth manifolds have been introduced and studied in geometry and topology (see
[
15
]
,
[
10
]
and
[
17
]
). In
[
5
]
, we produced a categorical generalization of principal bundles that makes sense in any site, i.e. in any category endowed with a Grothendieck topology. The topological group involved in the classical notion of principal bundle is generalized to a group object in the category. And the notion of locally trivial morphism is then internalized by considering pullbacks along the morphisms of a covering family for the Grothendieck topology. This generalized notion of principal bundle recovers the classical notions of principal bundles over topological spaces and smooth manifolds as particular cases.

In recent years, the need of a 2-dimensional notion of principal bundle has appeared in mathematical physics. The particular case of principal bundle over manifolds has been generalized to dimension 2 by Bartels, in their PhD thesis
[
2
]
. They introduced a notion of principal
G
-2-bundle over a smooth manifold, where
G
is a Lie 2-group and the smooth manifold is seen as a smooth 2-manifold with only identities. The main motivation arose from applications in higher gauge theory; instances of principal 2-bundles of this form can be found in topological quantum field theory and string theory.

In this paper, we introduce a notion of principal 2-bundle that makes sense in any nice enough 2-category equipped with a two-dimensional Grothendieck topology. More precisely, we use the notion of bitopology introduced by Street in
[
18
]
for bicategories. The internal groups used in the context of sites are here replaced by (coherent) 2-groups and pullbacks are replaced by iso-comma objects. Our notion of principal 2-bundle recovers Bartels’ one when considering the 2-category of differentiable categories, i.e. categories internal to the category of smooth manifolds, with the covering sieves generated by surjective local diffeomorphisms. We believe that our general theory of principal 2-bundles could have many applications both in higher gauge theory and in other mathematical contexts. It is also worth noticing that an analogous theory can be developed for bicategories using the same ideas.

Principal bundles over topological spaces, schemes or differentiable manifolds have also been used to perform an explicit construction of quotient stacks. Instances of this construction can be found in
[
16
]
, for the algebraic case, and in
[
9
]
, for the differentiable case. And in
[
5
]
, we performed an analogous construction in the categorical context of sites. In this paper, we generalize this construction to one dimension higher, producing particular trihomomorphisms from a
(2,1)
-category endowed with a bitopology into the tricategory
2\mathpzc{Cat}
of 2-categories. These trihomomorphisms are the analogues of quotient prestacks one dimension higher and will be thus called
quotient pre-2-stacks
.

Since the classical quotient prestacks are always stacks and the generalized quotient stacks of
[
5
]
are stacks if the site satisfies some mild conditions, it is natural to wonder whether generalized quotient pre-2-stacks satisfy stacky gluing conditions.
As there was no notion of higher dimensional stack suitable for quotient pre-2-stacks, in our paper
[
6
]
, we introduced a notion of 2-stack suitable for a trihomomorphism from a bisite into
\mathpzc{Bicat}
. Our definition naturally generalizes the definition of stack given by Street in
[
18
]
for pseudofunctors from a 2-category into
\mathpzc{Cat}
. But we also proved a useful characterization (Theorem 3.18 of
[
6
]
) in terms of explicit gluing conditions that can be checked more easily in practice. The obtained gluing conditions generalize one dimension higher the usual gluing conditions satisfied by a stack.

Analogously to what we showed in the one-dimensional case in
[
5
]
, we prove that, if the bitopology is subcanonical and the underlying
(2,1)
-category is nice enough, quotient pre-2-stacks are 2-stacks. We thus obtain a notion of quotient 2-stack. This is the main theorem of the paper.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\mathpzc{K}
be a bicocomplete
(2,1)
-category with finite flexible limits and such that iso-comma objects preserve bicolimits. Let then
\tau
be a subcanonical bitopology on
\mathpzc{K}
and
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G}\in\mathpzc{K}
with
\mathcal{G}
an internal 2-group. Then the quotient pre-2-stack
[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}]
is a 2-stack.

To prove this important result, we use the explicit characterization of 2-stack of our paper
[
6
]
, as well as the calculus of colimits in two-dimensional slices developed by Mesiti in
[
13
]
and
[
14
]
.

A key result needed for the proof of Theorem
1
is that every object of a subcanonical bisite can be expressed as some kind of two-dimensional colimit of each covering bisieve over it. We proved this result in
[
6
]
.

Quotient stacks are a fundamental concept in algebraic geometry because many stacks that naturally arise in geometric contexts either are quotient stacks or can be constructed using quotient stacks as building blocks. For this reason, we believe that both the generalized quotient stacks of
[
5
]
and the quotient 2-stacks studied in this paper could have many useful and interesting applications.

Furthermore, our categorical generalizations of principal bundles and quotient stacks could give a substantial contribution towards the development of a cohomology theory of schemes, and more in general of stacks, with coefficients in stacks of abelian 2-groups. This theory would produce new and refined 2-categorical invariants associated to schemes and algebraic stacks, that could solve numerous open problems in algebraic geometry.

Outline of the paper

In section
1
, we introduce and study principal 2-bundles. We start by recalling the notions of equivariant morphisms and 2-cells with respect to actions of internal (coherent) 2-groups. We then construct an action on the iso-comma object of two equivariant morphisms. Moreover, we introduce the notion of
2-locally trivial morphism
in a bisite. We use this notion to define
principal 2-bundles
and cells between them (Definition
1.15
). We conclude the section by proving the key result that principal 2-bundles are stable under iso-comma objects (Proposition
1.19
).

In section
2
, we use principal 2-bundles to explicitly construct quotient pre-2-stacks, restricting ourselves to the case of a
(2,1)
-category. We then prove that quotient pre-2-stacks are trihomomorphism into
2\mathpzc{Cat}
. Finally, we prove the main result of the paper, which is that, if the bisite is subcanonical and the underlying
(2,1)
-category satisfies mild assumptions, our quotient pre-2-stacks are 2-stacks (Theorem
2.14
).

1.
Principal 2-bundles

In this section we introduce a notion of principal 2-bundle in the context of bisites. This notion generalizes the notion of 2-space studied by Bartels in their PhD thesis
[
2
]
.

Throughout this section
\mathpzc{K}
will be a 2-category with all finite flexible limits and
\tau
will be a Grothendieck bitopology on it. The notion of bitopology used here is the one introduced by Street in
[
18
]
(and recalled also in our paper
[
6
]
).

Notation
.

Throughout this chapter the objects of
\mathpzc{K}
will be denoted by calligraphic letters instead of the usual capital block letters. This is coherent with the choice of other authors using the same concepts. And it is also helpful when comparing the notions presented here to the one-dimensional ones of
[
5
]
.

Remark 1.1
.

In Section
2
, we will restrict to the case in which
\mathpzc{K}
is a
(2,1)
-category as the invertibility of 2-cells is required to construct the higher dimensional analogues of quotient stacks.

Remark 1.2

(iso-comma objects)
.

Since
\mathpzc{K}
has all flexible limits (in the sense of
[
3
]
), it has in particular all iso-comma objects. If the iso-comma objects exists, it is a special representative of the bi-iso comma objects. For this reason, we will formulate some definitions that are bicategorical in nature and naturally involve bi-iso comma objects using iso-comma objects instead. This will offer substantial simplifications in the calculations. The same applies to biproducts and products.

We aim at introducing a notion of principal 2-bundle that makes sense in
\mathpzc{K}
. In this two-dimensional setting instead of an internal group we will consider an internal 2-group. This concept has been introduced by Baez and Lauda in
[
1
]
, but the idea had already been presented by Joyal and Street in
[
11
]
.

An internal 2-group in the 2-category
\mathpzc{K}
is an object of
\mathpzc{K}
endowed with a multiplication, a neutral element and an inverse morphism that satisfy associativity and unitality only up to fixed coherent 2-isomorphisms. We refer the reader to
[
1
]
for a complete definition.

We know recall the notion of action of an internal 2-group
\mathcal{G}\in\mathpzc{K}
on an object
\mathcal{X}\in\mathpzc{K}
. This definition can be found in
[
2
]
.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an internal
2
-group in
\mathpzc{K}
and let
\mathcal{X}
be an object of
\mathpzc{K}
. An
action
of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{X}
is given by a morphism

x\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}

together with invertible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}{}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

such that the following equalities of 2-cells hold:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptm\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptm\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt}\mu_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\circ\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{m}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}

).

Remark 1.4
.

For the action of an internal 2-group the usual axioms of action are true only up to coherent isomorphisms. These isomorphisms are
\mu_{x}
and
\nu_{x}
and their coherences are expressed by the equalities (
1
) and (
2
).

We now introduce the notions of
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphism
and

\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-cell
in
\mathpzc{K}
. These notions are well-known, but we could not find a reference for them in the literature.

Definition 1.5

(
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphism)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an internal 2-group in
\mathpzc{K}
that acts on the objects
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
of
\mathpzc{K}
with actions
x\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
and
y\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}\to Y
respectively. A morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is said
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant
if there exists an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}{}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

such that the following equalities hold:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptx}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{T}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

=
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{T}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

Remark 1.6
.

Notice that every morphism of
\mathpzc{K}
is
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant when the source and the target are equipped with trivial actions of
\mathcal{G}
. Having this in mind, we will always think of the objects of
\mathpzc{K}
as equipped with trivial actions of
\mathcal{G}
when the action is not specified.

Definition 1.7

(
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-cell)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an internal 2-group in
\mathpzc{K}
that acts on the objects
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
of
\mathpzc{K}
with actions
x\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
and
y\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}\to Y
respectively. Let
f,g\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphisms. A 2-cell
\gamma\colon f\Rightarrow g
is said
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant
if the following equality holds:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

The following result might be well-known, but we could not find a reference for it. It generalizes a well known fact about group objects to one dimension higher.

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
in
\mathpzc{K}
be an internal 2-group. The pseudofunctor

\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-\colon\mathpzc{K}\to\mathpzc{K}

is a
pseudomonad
with multiplication given by
m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{-}
and identity given by
e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{-}
.

Moreover, the pseudoalgebras for this pseudomonad are exactly the objects of
\mathpzc{K}
equipped by an action of
\mathcal{G}
; the pseudomorphisms between pseudoalgebras are exactly the
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphisms and the pseudotransformations between them are exactly the
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-cells.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-\colon\mathpzc{K}\to\mathpzc{K}
is a pseudomonad easily follows from the definition of internal 2-group. It is,then, straightforward to prove that the pseudoalgebras for this pseudomonad are exactly the objects of
\mathpzc{K}
equipped by an action of
\mathcal{G}
using the definition of action. Finally, the facts that pseudomorphisms between pseudoalgebras are exactly the
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphisms and that the pseudotransformations between them are exactly the
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-cells simply follow by the definitions of
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphisms and
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-cell respectively.
∎

We now describe how to define an action of
\mathcal{G}
on the iso-comma object of two morphisms in
\mathpzc{K}
, given actions of
\mathcal{G}
on the sources of the morphisms. This action will be largely used throughout the chapter.

Construction 1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an internal 2-group of
\mathpzc{K}
that acts on
\mathcal{P}\in\mathpzc{K}
with action
p\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
, on
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathpzc{K}
with action
y\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Y}
and on
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathpzc{K}
with action
z\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}
and let
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Y}
and
g\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
be
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphisms.

Consider, then, the iso-comma object square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}{}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

We want to define an action of
\mathcal{G}
on the iso-comma object
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
and we can do this using the morphism
\psi\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})\to\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
induced by the universal property of
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
as in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda_{g}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Proposition 1.10
.

The morphism
\psi\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})\to\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
defined in Construction
1.9
is an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
.

Proof.

We need to prove that there exist coherent invertible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}{}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

We induce
\mu_{\psi}
and
\nu_{\psi}
using the two-dimensional universal property of the iso-comma object
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
.

To induce
\mu_{\psi}
we consider the following isomorphic 2-cells:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf^{\ast}g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg^{\ast}f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptz}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}

Using the definition of
\psi
and the fact that
f
and
g
are
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant, it is straightforward to prove that these 2-cells are compatible and therefore they induce the desired isomorphic 2-cell
\mu_{\psi}
.

To induce
\nu_{\psi}
we consider the following isomorphic 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{T}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{T}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}

Using the definition of
\psi
and the fact that
f
and
g
are
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant, it is straightforward to prove that these 2-cells are compatible and therefore they induce the desired isomorphic 2-cell
\nu_{\psi}
.

It remains to prove that
\mu_{\psi}
and
\nu_{\psi}
satisfy the coherence conditions (
1
) and (
2
) of Definition
1.3
. This is straightforward using the fact that the corresponding coherences hold for the actions
p
and
z
.
∎

To define
principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles
, we will need to use the action induced by the internal multiplication of
\mathcal{G}
on a product of the form
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}
.

Proposition 1.11
.

The morphism

m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}

is an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

To simplify the notation we denote by
\rho
the morphism
m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}
. We need to prove that there exist coherent isomorphic 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}{}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

We induce
\mu_{\rho}
and
\nu_{\rho}
using the two-dimensional universal property of the product
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}
. To induce
\mu_{\rho}
we use the following isomorphic compatible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{m}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G})\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\rho}
\simeq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\simeq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

To induce
\nu_{\rho}
we use the following isomorphic compatible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{m}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{G})\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{X}}}
\simeq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

It remains to prove that
\mu_{\rho}
and
\nu_{\rho}
satisfy the coherence conditions (
1
) and (
2
) of Definition
1.3
. This can be easily done using the coherence diagrams satisfied by
m
and
e
respectively.
∎

In order to introduce
principal 2-bundles
, we need to define local triviality of a morphism with respect to the bitopology on
\mathpzc{K}
. The idea is to use iso-comma objects in place of the pullbacks used in the one-dimensional context.

Definition 1.12
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be objects of
\mathpzc{K}
with fixed actions of
\mathcal{G}
on them. We say that the morphism
g\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
is
2-locally trivial
if there exists a bisieve
S\colon R\Rightarrow\mathpzc{K}(-,\mathcal{X})
in
\tau(\mathcal{X})
such that, for every
f\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{X}
in the bisieve
S
, the iso-comma object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{X}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is equivalent to the product
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}
over
\mathcal{U}
via a
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant equivalence.

Remark 1.13

(iso-comma objects vs bi-iso-comma objects)
.

The previous definition could equivalently be given using bi-iso-comma objects instead of iso-comma objects. But since
\mathpzc{K}
has all iso-comma objects and they are representatives of the corresponding bi-iso-comma objects, we can more conveniently ask the required property for iso-comma objects. Notice that we only ask equivalences between the iso-comma objects and the corresponding products. This shows the bicategorical nature of the previous definition.

Remark 1.14

(reformulation of Definition
1.12
)
.

We can equivalently reformulate Definition
1.12
by asking the existence of a bisieve
S\colon R\Rightarrow\mathpzc{K}(-,\mathcal{X})
in
\tau(\mathcal{X})
such that, for every
f\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{X}
in the bisieve
S
, there exists a bi-iso-comma object of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

This reformulation will be useful for the proof of Proposition
1.19
.

We are now ready to introduce
principal 2-bundles
and cells between them.

Definition 1.15
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be an object of
\mathpzc{K}
. A
principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundle over
\mathcal{Y}
is an object
\mathcal{P}\in\mathpzc{K}
equipped with an action
p\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
and a
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-locally trivial morphism
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Y}
.

A
morphism of principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles over
\mathcal{Y}
from
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Y}
to
\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Y}
is a
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathpzc{K}
together with an isomorphic
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\varphi}}

\simeq

A
2-cell of principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles over
\mathcal{Y}
from
\varphi\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
to
\psi\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is a
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant 2-cell
\Gamma\colon\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
such that

\hbox to112.36pt{\vbox to94.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.18045pt\lower-36.6527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles over
\mathcal{Y}
together with morphisms and 2-cells between them form a 2-category that will be denoted
2\mathpzc{Bun}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{Y})
.

We now aim at proving that principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles are closed under iso-comma objects. This property is the higher dimensional analogue of the closure of principal bundles under pullbacks and it will be very important in order to construct quotient pre-2-stacks in Section
2
. To prove this crucial property, we will need to use the following result.

Proposition 1.16
.

The forgetful functor

U\colon\operatorname{Ps}\mbox{-}(\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-)\mbox{-}\operatorname{Alg}\to\mathpzc{K}

from the 2-category of objects of
\mathpzc{K}
equipped with actions of
\mathcal{G}
(that are the pseudoalgebras of the pseudomonad
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-
by Proposition
1.8
) to
\mathpzc{K}
lifts bi-iso-comma objects.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{P},p),(\mathcal{Z},z)
and
(\mathcal{Y},y)
be in
\operatorname{Ps}\mbox{-}(\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-)\mbox{-}\operatorname{Alg}
and let
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
be
\mathcal{G}
equivariant morphisms. Consider the bi-iso-comma object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

If
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
is endowed with an action of
\mathcal{G}
exactly as the one of Construction
1.9
(that is still an action in the case of the bi-iso-comma object), then
f^{\ast}g
and
g^{\ast}f
are
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant by definition of the action and so is the 2-cell
\lambda^{f,g}
.
Let now
(\mathcal{Q},q)
be in
\operatorname{Ps}\mbox{-}(\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-)\mbox{-}\operatorname{Alg}
and consider the morphism
v\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
induced by te universal property of the bi-iso-comma object
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
as in the following diagram

\hbox to142.63pt{\vbox to99.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 71.31238pt\lower-49.04709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

If
r,s
and
\Omega
are
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant, then so is the morphism
v
. Indeed, the invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Q}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}{}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

can be induced using the universal property of the bi-iso-comma
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
starting from the following compatible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega}^{-1}_{1}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\Omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pts}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega}_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\Omega^{-1}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptf^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

It is then easy to prove that
\alpha_{v}
satisfies the required coherence conditions.
Analogously, one can easily prove that if the starting data are in
\operatorname{Ps}\mbox{-}(\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-)\mbox{-}\operatorname{Alg}
, every 2-cell induced by the universal property of the bi-iso-comma object
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
is
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant. This conclude the proof that
U
lifts bi-iso-comma objects.
∎

Remark 1.17
.

The previous result of lifting requires an explicit proof as it does not easily follows from the pseudomonadicity of
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt-
(Proposition
1.8
). A result of lifting of PIE limits for pseudomonads is proved in
[
4
]
, but only for the forgetful functor from 2-category of strict algebras (with pseudomorphisms between them). This result is not strong enough for us as we cannot restrict to strict algebras. In fact, the strict algebras correspond with the actions that satisfy the usual properties of action on the nose.

We will also need to use the following well-known basic property of the bi-iso-comma object, which is a higher dimensional analogue of a well-known property of pullbacks.

Lemma 1.18
.

Given the following diagram in
\mathpzc{K}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where the isomorphic 2-cell
\lambda
exhibits a bi-iso-comma object, the isomorphic 2-cell
\beta
exhibits a bi-iso comma object if and only if the pasting diagram exhibits a bi-iso-comma object.

Proof.

Straightforward using the definition of bi-iso-comma object.
∎

We are now ready to prove that principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles are closed under iso-comma-objects.

Proposition 1.19
.

Let
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundle over
\mathcal{Y}
. Then the iso-comma object
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundle over
\mathcal{Z}
with morphism
\pi_{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}:=f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
1.16
, the morphism
f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
is
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant. So it remains to prove that it is 2-locally trivial.
Since
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
is 2-locally trivial, by Remark
1.14
, there exists a covering bisieve
S\colon R\Rightarrow\mathpzc{K}(-,\mathcal{Y})
over
\mathcal{Y}
such that, for every
g\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Y}
in the bisieve
S
, there exists a bi-iso-comma object of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

We now consider the covering bisieve
f^{\ast}S
. It is easy to see that every morphism in
f^{\ast}S
is isomorphic a composite

\mathcal{W}\xrightarrow{v}\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}\xrightarrow{f^{\ast}g}\mathcal{Z}

with
g\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
S
. And so it suffices to prove that there exists a bi-iso-comma object of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

We start observing that the identity 2-cell inside a commutative square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{T}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

exhibits a bi-iso-comma object. Indeed, it is straightforward to prove that
\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{U}
satisfies the universal properties of the bi-iso-comma object inducing the required morphisms and 2-cells via the universal properties of the product.
Then, thanks to Lemma
1.18
, it is enough to prove that there exists a bi-iso-comma object of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

To prove this, we first look at the following pasting of two bi-iso-comma objects

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057ptg^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

By Lemma
1.18
, it
exhibits a bi-iso comma object. Since the following 2-cell is invertible

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

the following pasting exhibits a bi-iso-comma object as well

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
\beta
is given by the pasting of
\hat{\delta}
and
\lambda^{f,g}
.

Moreover, by Lemma
1.18
, the following pasting of bi-iso-comma object squares

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{f}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

exhibits a bi-iso-comma object. Since both this bi-iso-comma object and that of diagram
3
are bi-iso-comma objects of
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
and
f\circ f^{\ast}g
, there exists an equivalence

\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{\kern-0.90417pt$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}{{\rightarrow}}{}\mathcal{M}

And it is straightforward to prove that also the following pasting diagram exhibits a bi-iso-comma object

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\sim

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{f}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where the isomorphic two-cell inside the left triangle is the one induced by the universal property of the bi-iso-comma object
\mathcal{M}
that induces the equivalence
\varphi
.

Finally, applying again Lemma
1.18
to diagram
5
(considering together the two 2-cells on the left), we conclude that the following diagram exhibits a bi-iso-comma object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}\hskip-0.43057pt\times\hskip-0.43057pt(\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\sim

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}g}

This concludes the proof that
f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
is a principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundle.
∎

2.
Quotient 2-stacks

In this section we will explicitly construct analogues of quotient stacks one dimension higher. We will then prove that they are well-defined trihomomorphisms. We will conclude the section proving that, under mild assumptions, these objects are 2-stacks. This will be the main result of the paper.

Throughout this section
\mathpzc{K}
will be a
(2,1)
-category with all finite flexible limits and
\tau
will be a bitopology on it. As we will explain in detail later on, we need to restrict ourselves to the case of a
(2,1)
category to be able to generalize the quotient prestacks of
[
5
]
to one dimension higher.

We start giving the explicit construction of
quotient pre-2-stacks
. It generalizes the definition of quotient prestacks of
[
5
]
to the two-dimensional context.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an object of the
(2,1)
-category
\mathpzc{K}
and let
\mathcal{G}
be an internal 2-group in
\mathpzc{K}
that acts on it. The
quotient pre-2-stack

[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}]\colon\mathpzc{K}^{\operatorname{op}}\to 2\mathpzc{Cat}

is defined as follows:

\bullet

for every object
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathpzc{K}
we define
[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](\mathcal{Y})
as the 2-category that has:

-

as objects the pairs
(\mathcal{P},\alpha)
where
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Y}
is a principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundle over
\mathcal{Y}
and
\alpha\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{X}
is a
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphism;

-

as morphisms from
(\mathcal{P},\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})
to
(\mathcal{Q},\alpha_{\mathcal{Q}})
the morphisms of principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles
\varphi\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
such that there exists an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\varphi}}

\simeq

-

as 2-cells from
\varphi\colon(\mathcal{P},\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})\to(\mathcal{Q},\alpha_{\mathcal{Q}})
to
\psi\colon(\mathcal{P},\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})\to(\mathcal{Q},\alpha_{\mathcal{Q}})
the 2-cells of principal
\mathcal{G}
-2-bundles
\Gamma\colon\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
such that

\hbox to112.36pt{\vbox to94.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.18045pt\lower-36.6527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\bullet

for every morphism
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
\mathpzc{K}
, we define

[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)\colon[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](\mathcal{Y})\to[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](\mathcal{Z})\vskip-9.95845pt

via iso-comma object along
f
as the 2-functor that sends:

-

an object
(\mathcal{P},\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})
to the pair
(\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z},\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{\ast}f)
, where
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
is given by the iso-comma object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and it is equipped with the morphism
f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
;

-

a morphism
\varphi\colon(\mathcal{P},\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})\to(\mathcal{Q},\alpha_{\mathcal{Q}})
to the morphism
[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\varphi)
induced by the universal property of the iso-comma object as in the following diagram

-

a 2-cell
\Gamma\colon\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
from
\varphi\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
to
\psi\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
to the 2-cell

[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\Gamma)\colon[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\varphi)\Rightarrow[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\psi)

induced by the two-dimensional universal property of the iso comma object
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
using the following compatible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\ast}f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}\hskip-0.43057pt\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}}

\bullet

for every 2-cell
\Lambda\colon f\Rightarrow g\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
in
\mathpzc{K}
, we define

[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](\Lambda)\colon[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](f)\Rightarrow[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](g)

as the 2-natural transformation that has component
[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](\Lambda)_{\mathcal{P}}
relative to
(\mathcal{P},\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})\in[{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{G}}](\mathcal{Y})
induced by the universal property of the iso-comma object
\mathcal{P}\hskip-0.43057pt{\times}^{g}_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip-0.43057pt\mathcal{Z}
as follows

\hbox to159.99pt{\vbox to101.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 79.43048pt\lower-47.806pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
